| *(didascalia)* | [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] shifting place [[Lysander]], speak again. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Thou runaway, thou coward, art thou fled?<br>Speak! In some bush? Where dost thou hide thy head? |
| *(didascalia)* | ROBIN shifting place |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Thou coward, art thou bragging to the [[Stars\|stars]],<br>Telling the bushes that thou look’st for wars,<br>And wilt not come? Come, recreant; come, thou [[Child\|child]],<br>I’ll whip thee with a rod. He is defiled<br>That draws a [[Sword\|sword]] on thee. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] shifting place Yea, art thou there? |
| *(didascalia)* | ROBIN shifting place |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Follow my voice; we’ll try no manhood here. Exeunt |
| *(didascalia)* | 3.3 Enter [[Lysander]] |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | He goes before me, and still dares me on;<br>When I come where he calls, then he is gone.<br>The [[Villain\|villain]] is much lighter heeled than I;<br>I followed fast, but faster he did fly,<br>That fallen am I in dark uneven way,<br>And here will rest me.<br>He lies down<br>Come, thou gentle day;<br>For if but once thou show me thy grey light,<br>I’ll find [[Demetrius]], and [[Revenge\|revenge]] this spite. He sleeps |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin Goodfellow and [[Demetrius]] |
| *(didascalia)* | ROBIN shifting place |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Ho, ho, ho, coward, why com’st thou not? |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Abide me if thou dar‘st, for [[Well\|well]] I wot<br>Thou runn’st before me, shifting every place,<br>And dar’st not stand nor look me in the face.<br>Where art thou now? |
| *(didascalia)* | ROBIN shifting place Come hither, I am here. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Nay, then thou mock’st me. Thou shalt buy this<br>dear<br>If ever I thy face by daylight see.<br>Now go thy way. Faintness constraineth me<br>To measure out my length on this cold bed.<br>He lies down<br>By day’s approach look to be visited. He sleeps |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Helena]] |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | O weary [[Night\|night]], O long and tedious [[Night\|night]],<br>Abate thy hours; shine comforts from the east<br>That I may back to Athens by daylight<br>From these that my poor company detest;<br>And [[Sleep\|sleep]], that sometimes shuts up sorrow’s eye,<br>Steal me a while from mine own company.<br>She lies down and sleeps |
| ROBIN | Yet but three? Come one more,<br>Two of both kinds makes up four. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Hermia]] |
| ROBIN | Here she comes, curst and sad.<br>Cupid is a knavish lad<br>Thus to make poor females mad. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | Never so weary, never so in woe,<br>Bedabbled with the dew, and torn with briers,<br>I can no further crawl, no further go.<br>My legs can keep no pace with my desires.<br>Here will I rest me till the break of day.<br>She lies down<br><br>Heavens shield [[Lysander]], if they mean a fray.<br>She sleeps |
| ROBIN | On the ground [[Sleep\|sleep]] sound.<br>I’ll apply to your eye,<br>Gentle [[Lover\|lover]], remedy.<br>He drops the juice on [[Lysander]]’s eyelids<br><br>When thou wak‘st thou tak’st<br>True delight in the sight<br>Of thy former lady’s eye,<br>And the country proverb known,<br>That ‘every man should take his own’,<br>In your waking shall be shown.<br>Jack shall have Jill,<br>Naught shall go ill,<br>the man shall have his mare again, and all shall be [[Well\|well]]. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Titania]], [[Queen]] of Fairies, and [[Bottom]] the clown with the ass-head, and fairies: Peaseblossom, Cobweb, Mote, and Mustardseed |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | (to [[Bottom]])<br>Come, sit thee down upon this flow’ry bed,<br>While I thy amiable cheeks do coy,<br>And stick musk-roses in thy sleek smooth head,<br>And kiss thy fair large ears, my gentle joy. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Where’s Peaseblossom? |
| PEASEBLOSSOM | Ready. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Scratch my head, Peaseblossom. Where’s Monsieur Cobweb? |
| COBWEB | Ready. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] Monsieur Cobweb, good monsieur, get you your weapons in your hand and kill me a red-hipped humble-bee on the top of a thistle; and, good monsieur, bring me the honeybag. Do not fret yourself too much in the action, monsieur; and, good monsieur, have a care the honeybag break not. I would be loath to have you overflowen with a honeybag, signor. Exit Cobweb Where’s Monsieur Mustardseed? |
| MUSTARDSEED | Ready. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Give me your neaf, Monsieur Mustardseed. Pray you, leave your courtesy, good monsieur. |
| MUSTARDSEED | What’s your will? |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Nothing, good monsieur, but to help Cavaliery Peaseblossom to scratch. I must to the barber’s, monsieur, for methinks I am marvellous hairy about the face; and I am such a tender ass, if my hair do but tickle me I must scratch. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | What, wilt thou hear some music, my sweet [[Love\|love]]? |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | I have a reasonable good ear in music. Let’s have the tongs and the bones. |
| *(didascalia)* | Rural music |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | Or say, sweet [[Love\|love]], what thou desir’st to eat. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Truly, a peck of provender. I could munch your good dry oats. Methinks I have a great [[Desire\|desire]] to a bottle of hay. Good hay, sweet hay, hath no fellow. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | I have a venturous fairy that shall seek<br>The squirrel’s hoard, and fetch thee off new nuts.<br>[[Bottom]] I had rather have a handful or two of dried peas. But I pray you, let none of your people stir me. I have an exposition of [[Sleep\|sleep]] come upon me. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | [[Sleep]] thou, and I will [[Wind\|wind]] thee in my arms.<br>Fairies, be gone, and be all ways away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Fairies |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | So doth the woodbine the sweet honeysuckle<br>Gently entwist; the female ivy so<br>Enrings the barky fingers of the elm.<br>O how I [[Love\|love]] thee, how I dote on thee! |
| *(didascalia)* | They [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin Goodfellow and [[Oberon]], meeting |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | Welcome, good Robin. Seest thou this sweet sight?<br>Her dotage now I do begin to pity,<br>For meeting her of late behind the wood,<br>Seeking sweet favours for this hateful [[Fool\|fool]],<br>I did upbraid her and fall out with her,<br>For she his hairy temples then had rounded<br>With coronet of fresh and fragrant [[Flowers\|flowers]],<br>And that same dew which sometime on the buds<br>Was wont to swell like round and [[Orient\|orient]] pearls<br>Stood now within the pretty flow’rets’ eyes,<br>Like [[Tears\|tears]] that did their own disgrace bewail.<br>When I had at my pleasure taunted her,<br>And she in mild terms begged my patience,<br>I then did ask of her her changeling [[Child\|child]],<br>Which straight she gave me, and her fairy sent<br>To bear him to my bower in fairyland.<br>And now I have the boy, I will undo<br>This hateful imperfection of her eyes.<br>And, gentle [[Puck\|puck]], take this transformed scalp<br>From off the head of this Athenian swain,<br>That he, awaking when the other do,<br>May all to Athens back again repair,<br>And think no more of this [[Night\|night]]’s accidents<br>But as the fierce vexation of [[A Dream\|a dream]].<br>But first I will release the Fairy [[Queen]].<br>He drops the juice on [[Titania]]’s eyelids<br>Be as thou wast wont to be,<br>See as thou wast wont to see.<br>Dian’s bud o’er Cupid’s flower<br>Hath such force and blessed power.<br>Now, my [[Titania]], wake you, my sweet [[Queen\|queen]]. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | (awaking)<br>My [[Oberon]], what visions have I seen!<br>Methought I was enamoured of an ass. |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | There lies your [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | How came these things to pass?<br>O, how mine eyes do loathe his visage now! |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | [[Silence]] a while.—Robin, take off this head.—[[Titania]], music call, and strike more dead Than common [[Sleep\|sleep]] of all these five the sense. |
| *(didascalia)* | Music, ho—music such as charmeth [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Still musica |
| ROBIN | (taking the ass-head off [[Bottom]])<br>Now when thou wak’st with thine own [[Fool\|fool]]’s eyes peep. |
| *(didascalia)* | Sound music. |
| *(didascalia)* | The music |
| *(didascalia)* | changes |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | Come, my [[Queen\|queen]], take hands with me,<br>And rock the ground whereon these sleepers be.<br>[[Oberon]] and [[Titania]] [[Dance\|dance]]<br><br>Now thou and I are new in amity,<br>And will tomorrow midnight solemnly<br>[[Dance]] in Duke [[Theseus]]’ [[house]], triumphantly,<br>And bless it to all fair prosperity.<br>There shall the pairs of faithful [[lovers]] be<br>Wedded with [[Theseus]], all in jollity. |
| ROBIN | Fairy [[King]], attend and mark.<br>I do hear the morning lark. |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | Then, my [[Queen\|queen]], in [[Silence\|silence]] sad<br>Trip we after nightës shade.<br>We the globe can compass soon,<br>Swifter than the wand’ring [[Moon\|moon]]. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | Come, my lord, and in our flight<br>Tell me how it came this [[Night\|night]]<br>That I sleeping here was found<br>With these mortals on the ground. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Oberon]], [[Titania]], and Robin. The sleepers lie still [[Wind]] horns within. Enter [[Theseus]] with Egeus, [[Hippolyta]], and all his train |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Go, one of you, find out the forester,<br>For now our observation is performed;<br>And since we have the vanguard of the day,<br>My [[Love\|love]] shall hear the music of my hounds.<br>Uncouple in the western valley; let them go.<br>Dispatch, I say, and find the forester. Exit one<br><br>We will, fair [[Queen]], up to the mountain’s top,<br>And mark the musical confusion<br>Of hounds and echo in conjunction. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | I was with Hercules and Cadmus once<br>When in a wood of Crete they bayed the bear<br>With hounds of Sparta. Never did I hear<br>Such gallant chiding; for besides the groves,<br>The skies, the fountains, every region near<br>Seemed all one mutual cry. I never heard<br>So musical a discord, such sweet thunder. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | My hounds are bred out of the Spartan kind,<br>So flewed, so sanded; and their heads are hung<br>With ears that sweep away the morning dew,<br>Crook-kneed, and dewlapped like Thessalian bulls,<br>Slow in pursuit, but matched in mouth like [[Bells\|bells]],<br>Each under each. A cry more tuneable<br>Was never holla’d to nor cheered with horn<br>In Crete, in Sparta, nor in Thessaly.<br>Judge when you hear. But soft: what nymphs are<br>these? |
| EGEUS | My lord, this is my daughter here asleep,<br>And this [[Lysander]]; this [[Demetrius]] is;<br>This [[Helena]], old Nedar’s [[Helena]].<br>I [[Wonder\|wonder]] of their being here together. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | No [[Doubt\|doubt]] they [[Rose\|rose]] up early to observe<br>The rite of May, and, hearing our intent,<br>Came here in grace of our solemnity.<br>But speak, Egeus : is not this the day<br>That [[Hermia]] should give answer of her choice? |
| EGEUS | It is, my lord. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Go bid the huntsmen wake them with their horns. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit one |
| *(didascalia)* | Shout within: [[Wind\|wind]] horns. The [[lovers]] all start up |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Good morrow, friends. Saint [[Valentine]] is past.<br>Begin these wood-[[Birds\|birds]] but to couple now? |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Pardon, my lord.<br>The [[lovers]] kneel |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | I pray you all stand up.<br>The [[lovers]] stand<br>(To [[Demetrius]] and [[Lysander]]) I know you two are rival<br>enemies.<br>How comes this gentle concord in the world,<br>That hatred is so far from [[Jealousy\|jealousy]]<br>To [[Sleep\|sleep]] by hate, and [[Fear\|fear]] no enmity? |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | My lord, I shall reply amazèdly,<br>Half [[Sleep\|sleep]], half waking. But as yet, I swear,<br>I cannot truly say how I came here,<br>But as I think—for truly would I speak,<br>And, now I do bethink me, so it is—<br>I came with [[Hermia]] hither. Our intent<br>Was to be gone from Athens where we might,<br>Without the peril of the Athenian law— |
| EGEUS | (to<br>[[Theseus]])<br>Enough, enough, my lord, you have enough.<br>I beg the law, the law upon his head.—<br>They would have stol’n away, they would, [[Demetrius]],<br>Thereby to have defeated you and me—<br>You of your wife, and me of my consent,<br>Of my consent that she should be your wife. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | (to [[Theseus]])<br>My lord, fair [[Helen]] told me of their stealth,<br>Of this their purpose hither to this wood,<br>And I in fury hither followed them,<br>Fair [[Helena]] in fancy following me.<br>But, my good lord, I wot not by what power—<br>But by some power it is—my [[Love\|love]] to [[Hermia]],<br>Melted as the [[Snow\|snow]], seems to me now<br>As the remembrance of an idle gaud<br>Which in my [[Childhood\|childhood]] I did’dote upon,<br>And all the [[Faith\|faith]], the virtue of my heart,<br>The object and the pleasure of mine eye<br>Is only [[Helena]]. To her, my lord,<br>Was I betrothed ere I see [[Hermia]].<br>But like in sickness did I loathe this food;<br>But, as in health come to my natural taste,<br>Now I do wish it, [[Love\|love]] it, long for it,<br>And will for evermore be true to it. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Fair [[lovers]], you are fortunately met.<br>Of this discourse we more will hear anon.—<br>Egeus, I will overbear your will,<br>For in the temple by and by with us<br>These couples shall eternally be knit.—<br>And, for the morning now is something worn,<br>Our purposed hunting shall be set aside.<br>Away with us to Athens. Three and three,<br>We’ll hold a feast in great solemnity.<br>Come, [[Hippolyta]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Duke [[Theseus]] with [[Hippolyta]], Egeus, and all his train |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | These things seem small and undistinguishable,<br>Like far-off [[Mountains\|mountains]] turned into clouds. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | Methinks I see these things with parted eye,<br>When everything seems [[Double\|double]]. |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | So methinks,<br>And I have found [[Demetrius]] like a [[Jewel\|jewel]],<br>Mine own and not mine own. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | It seems to me<br>That yet we [[Sleep\|sleep]], we dream. Do not you think<br>The Duke was here and bid us follow him? |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | Yea, and my father. |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | And [[Hippolyta]]. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | And he did bid us follow to the temple. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Why then, we are awake. Let’s follow him,<br>And by the way let us recount our [[Dreams\|dreams]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the [[lovers]] |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | [[Bottom]] wakes |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | When my cue comes, call me, and I will answer. My next is ‘most fair Pyramus’. Heigh-ho. Peter Quince? Flute the bellows-mender? Snout the tinker? Starveling? God’s my life! Stolen hence, and left me asleep?—I have had a most rare vision. I have had [[A Dream\|a dream]] past the wit of man to say what dream it was. Man is but an ass if he go about t‘expound this dream. Methought I was—there is no man can tell what. Methought I was, and methought I had—but man is but a patched [[Fool\|fool]] if he will offer to say what methought I had. The eye of man hath not heard, the ear of man hath not seen, man’s hand is not able to taste, his tongue to conceive, nor his heart to report what my dream was. I will get Peter Quince to write a ballad of this dream. It shall be called ‘[[Bottom]]’s Dream’, because it hath no [[Bottom\|bottom]], and I will sing it in the latter end of a play, before the Duke. Peradventure, to make it the more gracious, I shall sing it at her [[Death\|death]]. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Quince, Flute, Snout, and Starveling |
| QUINCE | Have you sent to [[Bottom]]’s [[house]]? Is he come [[Home\|home]] yet? |
| STARVELING | He cannot be heard of. Out of [[Doubt\|doubt]] he is transported. |
| FLUTE | If he come not, then the play is marred. It goes not forward. Doth it? |
| QUINCE | It is not possible. You have not a man in all Athens able to discharge Pyramus but he. |
| FLUTE | No, he hath simply the best wit of any handicraft-man in Athens. |
| QUINCE | Yea, and the best person, too; and he is a very paramour for a sweet voice. |
| FLUTE | You must say ‘paragon’. A paramour is, God bless us, a thing of naught. Enter Snug the joiner |
| SNUG | Masters, the Duke is coming from the temple, and there is two or three lords and ladies more married. If our sport had gone forward we had all been made men. |
| FLUTE | O sweet bully [[Bottom]]! Thus hath he lost sixpence a day during his life. He could not have scaped sixpence a day. An the Duke had not given him sixpence a day for playing Pyramus, I’ll be hanged. He would have deserved it. Sixpence a day in Pyramus, or nothing. Enter [[Bottom]] |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Where are these lads? Where are these hearts? |
| QUINCE | [[Bottom]]! O most courageous day! O most happy hour! |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Masters, I am to discourse wonders; but ask me not what. For if I tell you, I am no true Athenian. I will tell you everything right as it fell out. |
| QUINCE | Let us hear, sweet [[Bottom]]. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Not a word of me. All that I will tell you is that the Duke hath dined. Get your apparel together, good strings to your beards, new ribbons to your pumps. Meet presently at the palace; every man look o’er his part. For the short and the long is, our play is preferred. In any case let Thisbe have clean linen, and let not him that plays the [[Lion\|lion]] pare his nails, for they shall hang out for the [[Lion\|lion]]’s claws. And, most dear actors, eat no onions nor garlic, for we are to utter sweet breath, and I do not [[Doubt\|doubt]] but to hear them say it is a sweet [[Comedy\|comedy]]. No more words. Away, go, away! Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Theseus]], |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Hippolyta]], Egeus, and |
| *(didascalia)* | attendant lords |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | ’Tis strange, my [[Theseus]], that these [[lovers]] speak of. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | More strange than true. I never may believe<br>These antique fables, nor these fairy toys.<br>[[lovers\|Lovers]] and madmen have such seething brains,<br>Such shaping fantasies, that apprehend<br>More than cool reason ever comprehends.<br>The lunatic, the [[Lover\|lover]], and the poet<br>Are of [[Imagination\|imagination]] all compact.<br>One sees more devils than vast hell can hold:<br>That is the madman. The [[Lover\|lover]], all as frantic,<br>Sees [[Helen]]’s [[Beauty\|beauty]] in a brow of [[Egypt]].<br>The poet’s eye, in a fine frenzy [[rolling]],<br>Doth glance from heaven to earth, from earth to<br>heaven,<br>And as [[Imagination\|imagination]] bodies forth<br>The forms of things unknown, the poet’s pen<br>Turns them to shapes, and gives to airy nothing<br>A local habitation and a name.<br>Such tricks hath strong [[Imagination\|imagination]]<br>That if it would but apprehend some joy<br>It comprehends some bringer of that joy;<br>Or in the [[Night\|night]], imagining some [[Fear\|fear]],<br>How easy is a bush supposed a bear! |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | But all the story of the [[Night\|night]] told over,<br>And all their minds transfigured so together,<br>More witnesseth than fancy’s images,<br>And grows to something of great constancy;<br>But howsoever, strange and admirable. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[lovers]]: [[Lysander]], |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | [[Demetrius]], [[Hermia]], and [[Helena]] |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Here come the [[lovers]], full of joy and mirth.<br>Joy, gentle friends—joy and fresh days of [[Love\|love]]<br>Accompany your hearts. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | More than to us<br>Wait in your royal walks, your board, your bed. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Come now, what masques, what dances shall we have<br>To wear away this long age of three hours<br>Between our after-supper and bed-[[Time\|time]]?<br>Where is our usual manager of mirth?<br>What revels are in hand? Is there no play<br>To ease the anguish of a torturing hour?<br>Call Egeus. |
| *(didascalia)* | REGEUS Here, mighty [[Theseus]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Say, what abridgement have you for this evening?<br>What [[Masque\|masque]], what music? How shall we beguile<br>The lazy [[Time\|time]] if not with some delight? |
| *(didascalia)* | EGEUS |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | There is a brief how many sports are ripe.<br>Make choice of which your highness will see first. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] (reads) |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ‘The battle with the centaurs, to be sung<br>By an Athenian eunuch to the harp.’ |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | We’ll none of that. That have I told my [[Love\|love]]<br>In glory of my kinsman Hercules. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] (reads) |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ‘The riot of the tipsy bacchanals<br>Tearing the Thracian singer in their rage.’ |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | That is an old device, and it was played<br>When I from Thebes came last a conqueror. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] (reads) |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ‘The thrice-three muses mourning for the [[Death\|death]]<br>Of learning, late deceased in beggary.’ |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | That is some [[Satire\|satire]], keen and critical,<br>Not sorting with a nuptial ceremony. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] (reads) |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ‘A tedious brief scene of young Pyramus<br>And his [[Love\|love]] Thisbe: very tragical mirth.’ |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ‘Merry’ and ‘tragical’? ‘Tedious’ and ’brief?—<br>That is, hot ice and wondrous strange black [[Snow\|snow]].<br>How shall we find the concord of this discord? |
| *(didascalia)* | EGEUS |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | A play there is, my lord, some ten words long,<br>Which is as ‘brief’ as I have known a play;<br>But by ten words, my lord, it is too long,<br>Which makes it ‘tedious’; for in all the play<br>There is not one word apt, one player fitted.<br>And ‘tragical’, my noble lord, it is,<br>For Pyramus therein doth kill himself;<br>Which when I saw rehearsed, I must confess,<br>Made mine eyes [[Water\|water]]; but more merry [[Tears\|tears]]<br>The passion of loud laughter never shed. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | What are they that do play it? |
| *(didascalia)* | EGEUS |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Hard-handed men that work in Athens here,<br>Which never laboured in their minds till now,<br>And now have toiled their unbreathed memories<br>With this same play against your nuptial. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | And we will hear it. |
| *(didascalia)* | EGEUS No, my noble lord, |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | It is not for you. I have heard it over,<br>And it is nothing, nothing in the world,<br>Unless you can find sport in their intents<br>Extremely stretched, and conned with cruel [[Pain\|pain]]<br>To do you service. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | I will hear that play;<br>For never anything can be amiss<br>When simpleness and duty tender it.<br>Go, bring them in; and take your places, ladies. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Egeus |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | I [[Love\|love]] not to see wretchedness o’ercharged,<br>And duty in his service perishing. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Why, gentle sweet, you shall see no such thing. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | He says they can do nothing in this kind. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | The kinder we, to give them thanks for nothing.<br>Our sport shall be to take what they mistake,<br>And what poor duty cannot do,<br>Noble respect takes it in might, not merit.<br>Where I have come, great clerks have purposed<br>To greet me with premeditated welcomes,<br>Where I have seen them shiver and look pale,<br>Make periods in the midst of sentences,<br>Throttle their practised accent in their fears,<br>And in conclusion dumbly have broke off,<br>Not paying me a welcome. Trust me, sweet,<br>Out of this [[Silence\|silence]] yet I picked a welcome,<br>And in the modesty of fearful duty<br>I read as much as from the rattling tongue<br>Of saucy and audacious eloquence.<br>[[Love]], therefore, and tongue-tied simplicity<br>In least speak most, to my capacity. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Egeus |
| *(didascalia)* | EGEUS |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | So please your grace, the Prologue is addressed. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Let him approach. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish trumpets. Enter Quince as the Prologue |
| *(didascalia)* | QUINCE (as Prologue) |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | If we offend, it is with our good will.<br>That you should think: we come not to offend<br>But with good will. To show our simple skill,<br>That is the true beginning of our end.<br>Consider then we come but in despite.<br>We do not come as minding to content you,<br>Our true intent is. All for your delight<br>We are not here. That you should here repent you<br>The actors are at hand, and by their show<br>You shall know all that you are like to know. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | This fellow doth not stand upon points. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | He hath rid his prologue like a rough colt: he knows not the stop. A good moral, my lord: it is not enough to speak, but to speak true. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Indeed, he hath played on this prologue like a [[Child\|child]] on a recorder—a sound, but not in [[government]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | His speech was like a tangled chain—nothing impaired, but all disordered. Who is next? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter with a trumpeter before them [[Bottom]] as Pyramus, Flute as Thisbe, Snout as Wall, Starveling as Moonshine, and Snug as [[Lion]], for the dumb show |
| *(didascalia)* | QUINCE (as Prologue) |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Gentles, perchance you [[Wonder\|wonder]] at this show,<br>But [[Wonder\|wonder]] on, till [[Truth\|truth]] make all things plain.<br>This man is Pyramus, if you would know;<br>This beauteous lady Thisbe is, certain.<br>This man with lime and roughcast doth present<br>Wall, that vile wall which did these [[lovers]] sunder;<br>And through Wall’s chink, poor souls, they are content<br>To whisper; at the which let no man [[Wonder\|wonder]].<br>This man, with lantern, dog, and bush of thorn,<br>Presenteth Moonshine. For if you will know,<br>By moonshine did these [[lovers]] think no scorn<br>To meet at Ninus’ tomb, there, there to woo.<br>This grizzly beast, which ’[[Lion]]’ hight by name,<br>The trusty Thisbe coming first by [[Night\|night]]<br>Did scare away, or rather did affright;<br>And as she fled, her mantle she did fall,<br>Which [[Lion]] vile with bloody mouth did stain.<br>Anon comes Pyramus, sweet youth and tall,<br>And finds his trusty Thisbe’s mantle slain;<br>Whereat with blade—with bloody, blameful blade—<br>He bravely broached his boiling bloody breast;<br>And Thisbe, tarrying in mulberry shade,<br>His dagger drew and died. For all the rest,<br>Let [[Lion]], Moonshine, Wall, and [[lovers]] twain<br>At large discourse, while here they do remain. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all the clowns but Snout as Wall |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | I [[Wonder\|wonder]] if the [[Lion\|lion]] be to speak. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | No [[Wonder\|wonder]], my lord—one [[Lion\|lion]] may when many asses do. |
| *(didascalia)* | SNOUT (as Wall) |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | In this same interlude it doth befall<br>That I, one Snout by name, present a wall;<br>And such a wall as I would have you think<br>That had in it a crannied hole or chink,<br>Through which the [[lovers]] Pyramus and Thisbe<br>Did whisper often, very secretly.<br>This loam, this roughcast, and this stone doth show<br>That I am that same wall; the [[Truth\|truth]] is so.<br>And this the cranny is, right and sinister,<br>Through which the fearful [[lovers]] are to whisper. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Would you [[Desire\|desire]] lime and hair to speak better? |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | It is the wittiest partition that ever I heard discourse, my lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Bottom]] as Pyramus |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Pyramus draws near the wall. [[Silence]]. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>O grim-looked [[Night\|night]], O [[Night\|night]] with hue so black,<br>O [[Night\|night]] which ever art when day is not;<br>O [[Night\|night]], O [[Night\|night]], alack, alack, alack,<br>I [[Fear\|fear]] my Thisbe’s promise is forgot.<br>And thou, O wall, O sweet O lovely wall,<br>That stand’st between her father’s ground and mine,<br>Thou wall, O wall, O sweet and lovely wall,<br>Show me thy chink, to blink through with mine<br>eyne.<br>Wall shows his chink<br>Thanks, courteous wall. Jove shield thee [[Well\|well]] for this.<br>But what see I? No Thisbe do I see.<br>O wicked wall, through whom I see no bliss,<br>Cursed be thy stones for thus deceiving me. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | The wall methinks, being sensible, should curse again. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (to [[Theseus]]) No, in [[Truth\|truth]], sir, he should not.<br>’Deceiving me’ is Thisbe’s cue. She is to enter now,<br>and I am to spy her through the wall. You shall see,<br>it will fall pat as I told you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Flute as Thisbe |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Yonder she comes. |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>O wall, full often hast thou heard my moans<br>For parting my fair Pyramus and me.<br>My cherry lips have often kissed thy stones,<br>Thy stones with lime and hair knit up in thee. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>I see a voice. Now will I to the chink<br>To spy an I can hear my Thisbe’s face.<br>Thisbe? |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe) My [[Love\|love]]—thou art my [[Love\|love]], I think. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>Think what thou wilt, I am thy [[Lover\|lover]]’s grace,<br>And like Lemander am I trusty still. |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>And I like [[Helen]], till the fates me kill. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>Not Shaphalus to Procrus was so true. |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>As Shaphalus to Procrus, I to you. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>O kiss me through the hole of this vile wall. |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>I kiss the wall’s hole, not your lips at all. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>Wilt thou at Ninny’s tomb meet me straightway? |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>Tide life, tide [[Death\|death]], I come without delay. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[Bottom]] and Flute severally |
| SNOUT | (as Wall)<br>Thus have I, Wall, my part discharged so;<br>And being done, thus Wall away doth go. Exit |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Now is the wall down between the two neighbours. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | No remedy, my lord, when walls are so wilful to hear without warning. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | This is the silliest stuff that ever I heard. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | The best in this kind are but shadows, and the worst are no worse if [[Imagination\|imagination]] amend them. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | It must be your [[Imagination\|imagination]], then, and not theirs. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | If we imagine no worse of them than they of themselves, they may pass for excellent men. Here come two noble beasts in: a man and a [[Lion\|lion]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Snug as [[Lion]], and Starveling as Moonshine with a lantern, thorn bush, and dog |
| SNUG | (as [[Lion]])<br>You, ladies, you whose gentle hearts do [[Fear\|fear]]<br>The smallest monstrous mouse that creeps on floor,<br>May now perchance both quake and tremble here<br>When [[Lion\|lion]] rough in wildest rage doth roar.<br>Then know that I as Snug the joiner am<br>A [[Lion\|lion]] fell, nor else no [[Lion\|lion]]’s dam.<br>For if I should as [[Lion]] come in strife<br>Into this place, ’twere pity on my life. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | A very gentle beast, and of a good [[Conscience\|conscience]]. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | The very best at a beast, my lord, that e’er I saw. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | This [[Lion\|lion]] is a very fox for his valour. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | True, and a goose for his discretion. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Not so, my lord, for his valour cannot carry his discretion, and the fox carries the goose. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | His discretion, I am sure, cannot carry his valour, for the goose carries not the fox. It is [[Well\|well]]. Leave it to his discretion, and let us listen to the [[Moon\|moon]]. |
| STARVELING | (as Moonshine) This lantern doth the hornèd [[Moon\|moon]] present. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | He should have worn the horns on his head. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | He is no crescent, and his horns are invisible within the circumference. |
| STARVELING | (as Moonshine)<br>This lantern doth the hornèd [[Moon\|moon]] present.<br>Myself the man i’th’ [[Moon\|moon]] do seem to be. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | This is the greatest error of all the rest—the man should be put into the lantern. How is it else the man i’th’ [[Moon\|moon]]? |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | He dares not come there for the candle; for you see it is already in snuff. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | I am aweary of this [[Moon\|moon]]. Would he would change. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | It appears by his small light of discretion that he is in the wane; but yet in courtesy, in all reason, we must stay the [[Time\|time]]. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Proceed, [[Moon]]. |
| STARVELING | All that I have to say is to tell you that the lantern is the [[Moon\|moon]], I the man i’th’ [[Moon\|moon]], this thorn bush my thorn bush, and this dog my dog. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Why, all these should be in the lantern, for all these are in the [[Moon\|moon]]. But [[Silence\|silence]]; here comes Thisbe. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Flute as Thisbe |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>This is old Ninny’s tomb. Where is my [[Love\|love]]? |
| SNUG | (as [[Lion]]) O.<br>[[Lion]] roars. Thisbe drops her mantle and runs off |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | [[Well]] roared, [[Lion]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | [[Well]] run, Thisbe. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | [[Well]] shone, [[Moon]].—Truly, the [[Moon\|moon]] shines with a good grace.<br>[[Lion]] worries Thisbe’s mantle |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | [[Well]] moused, [[Lion]]. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | And then came Pyramus. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Bottom]] as Pyramus |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] And so the [[Lion\|lion]] vanished. Exit [[Lion]] |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>Sweet [[Moon\|moon]], I thank thee for thy sunny beams.<br>I thank thee, [[Moon\|moon]], for shining now so bright;<br>For by thy gracious, golden, glittering gleams<br>I trust to take of truest Thisbe sight.<br>But stay, O spite!<br>But mark, poor knight,<br>What dreadful dole is here?<br>Eyes, do you see?<br>How can it be?<br>O dainty duck, O dear!<br>Thy mantle good,<br>What, stained with [[Blood\|blood]]?<br>Approach, ye furies fell.<br>O fates, come, come,<br>Cut thread and thrum,<br>Quail, crush, conclude, and quell. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | This passion—and the [[Death\|death]] of a dear friend—would go near to make a man look sad. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Beshrew my heart, but I pity the man. |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | (as Pyramus)<br>O wherefore, [[Nature\|nature]], didst thou lions frame,<br>Since [[Lion\|lion]] vile hath here deflowered my dear?—<br>Which is—no, no, which was—the fairest dame<br>That lived, that loved, that liked, that looked, with<br>cheer.<br>Come [[Tears\|tears]], confound;<br>Out [[Sword\|sword]], and wound<br>The pap of Pyramus.<br>Ay, that left pap,<br>Where heart doth hop.<br>Thus die I: thus, thus, thus.<br>He stabs himself<br><br>Now am I dead,<br>Now am I fled,<br>My [[Soul\|soul]] is in the sky.<br>Tongue, lose thy light; |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Moon]], take thy flight. Exit Moonshine |
| [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] | Now die, die, die, die, die. He dies |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | No die but an ace for him; for he is but one. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Less than an ace, man; for he is dead; he is nothing. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | With the help of a surgeon he might yet recover and prove an ass. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | How chance Moonshine is gone before Thisbe comes back and finds her [[Lover\|lover]]? |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | She will find him by starlight. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Flute as Thisbe |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Here she comes, and her passion ends the play. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Methinks she should not use a long one for such a Pyramus. I [[Hope\|hope]] she will be brief. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | A mote will turn the balance which Pyramus, which Thisbe, is the better—he for a man, God warrant us; she for a woman, God bless us. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | She hath spied him already with those sweet eyes. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | And thus she means, videlicet: |
| FLUTE | (as Thisbe)<br>Asleep, my [[Love\|love]]?<br>What, dead, my dove?<br>O Pyramus, arise.<br>Speak, speak. Quite dumb?<br>Dead, dead? A tomb<br>Must cover thy sweet eyes.<br>These lily lips,<br>This cherry nose,<br>These yellow cowslip cheeks<br>Are gone, are gone.<br>[[lovers\|Lovers]], make moan.<br>His eyes were green as leeks.<br>O sisters three,<br>Come, come to me<br>With hands as pale as milk.<br>Lay them in gore,<br>Since you have shore<br>With shears his thread of silk.<br>Tongue, not a word.<br>Come, trusty [[Sword\|sword]],<br>Come, blade, my breast imbrue.<br>She stabs herself<br><br>And farewell friends,<br>Thus Thisbe ends.<br>Adieu, adieu, adieu. She dies |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Moonshine and [[Lion]] are left to bury the dead. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Ay, and Wall too. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Bottom\|BOTTOM]] No, I assure you, the wall is down that parted their fathers. Will it please you to see the epilogue or to hear a bergamask [[Dance\|dance]] between two of our company? |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | No epilogue, I pray you; for your play needs no excuse. Never excuse; for when the players are all dead there need none to be blamed. Marry, if he that writ it had played Pyramus and hanged himself in Thisbe’s garter it would have been a fine [[Tragedy\|tragedy]]; and so it is, truly, and very notably discharged. But come, your bergamask. Let your epilogue [[Alone\|alone]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Bottom]] |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | and |
| *(didascalia)* | Flute [[Dance\|dance]] a bergamask, then exeunt |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | The iron tongue of midnight hath told twelve. [[lovers\|Lovers]], to bed; ’tis almost fairy [[Time\|time]]. I [[Fear\|fear]] we shall outsleep the coming morn As much as we this [[Night\|night]] have overwatched. This palpable-gross play hath [[Well\|well]] beguiled The heavy gait of [[Night\|night]]. Sweet friends, to bed. A fortnight hold we this solemnity In nightly revels and new jollity. Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin Goodfellow with a broom |
| ROBIN | Now the hungry [[Lion\|lion]] roars,<br>And the wolf behowls the [[Moon\|moon]],<br>Whilst the heavy ploughman snores,<br>All with weary task fordone.<br>Now the wasted brands do glow<br>Whilst the screech-owl, screeching loud,<br>Puts the wretch that lies in woe<br>In remembrance of a shroud.<br>Now it is the [[Time\|time]] of [[Night\|night]]<br>That the graves, all gaping wide,<br>Every one lets forth his sprite<br>In the churchway paths to glide;<br>And we fairies that do run<br>By the triple Hecate’s team<br>From the presence of the sun,<br>Following darkness like [[A Dream\|a dream]],<br>Now are frolic. Not a mouse<br>Shall disturb this hallowed [[house]].<br>I am sent with broom before<br>To sweep the [[Dust\|dust]] behind the door. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Oberon]] and [[Titania]], [[King]] and [[Queen]] of Fairies, with all their train |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | Through the [[house]] give glimmering light.<br>By the dead and drowsy [[Fire\|fire]]<br>Every elf and fairy sprite<br>Hop as light as bird from brier,<br>And this ditty after me<br>Sing, and [[Dance\|dance]] it trippingly. |
| [[Titania\|TITANIA]] | First rehearse your [[Song\|song]] by rote,<br>To each word a warbling note.<br>Hand in hand with fairy grace<br>Will we sing and bless this place. |
| *(didascalia)* | The [[Song\|song]]. The fairies [[Dance\|dance]] |
| [[Oberon\|OBERON]] | Now until the break of day<br>Through this [[house]] each fairy stray.<br>To the best bride bed will we,<br>Which by us shall blessèd be,<br>And the issue there create<br>Ever shall be fortunate.<br>So shall all the couples three<br>Ever true in loving be,<br>And the blots of [[Nature\|nature]]’s hand<br>Shall not in their issue stand.<br>Never mole, harelip, nor scar,<br>Nor mark prodigious such as are<br>Despised in nativity<br>Shall upon their children be.<br>With this field-dew consecrate<br>Every fairy take his gait<br>And each several chamber bless<br>Through this palace with sweet peace;<br>And the owner of it blessed<br>Ever shall in safety rest.<br>Trip away, make no stay,<br>Meet me all by break of day. Exeunt all but Robin<br><br>Epilogue |
| ROBIN | If we shadows have offended,<br>Think but this, and all is mended:<br>That you have but slumbered here,<br>While these visions did appear;<br>And this weak and idle theme,<br>No more yielding but [[A Dream\|a dream]],<br>Gentles, do not reprehend.<br>If you pardon, we will mend.<br>And as I am an honest [[Puck\|puck]],<br>If we have unearned luck<br>Now to ’scape the serpent’s tongue,<br>We will make amends ere long,<br>Else the [[Puck\|puck]] a liar call.<br>So, good [[Night\|night]] unto you all.<br>Give me your hands, if we be friends,<br>And Robin shall restore amends. |
| ADDITIONAL PASSAGES | An unusual quantity and kind of mislineation in the first edition has persuaded most scholars that the text at the beginning of 5.1 was revised, with new material written in the margins. We here offer a reconstruction of the passage as originally drafted, which can be compared with 5.1.1―86 of the edited text. |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Theseus]], [[Hippolyta]], and Philostrate |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | ’Tis strange, my [[Theseus]], that these [[lovers]] speak of. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | More strange than true. I never may believe<br>These antique fables, nor these fairy toys.<br>[[lovers\|Lovers]] and mad men have such seething brains.<br>One sees more devils than vast hell can hold: 5<br>That is the madman. The [[Lover\|lover]], all as frantic,<br>Sees [[Helen]]’s [[Beauty\|beauty]] in a brow of [[Egypt]].<br>Such tricks hath strong [[Imagination\|imagination]]<br>That if it would but apprehend some joy<br>It comprehends some bringer of that joy; 10<br>Or in the [[Night\|night]], imagining some [[Fear\|fear]],<br>How easy is a bush supposed a bear! |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | But all the story of the [[Night\|night]] told over,<br>And all their minds transfigured so together,<br>More witnesseth than fancy’s images, 15<br>And grows to something of great constancy;<br>But howsoever, strange and admirable. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[lovers]]: [[Lysander]], [[Demetrius]], [[Hermia]], and [[Helena]] |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Here come the [[lovers]], full of joy and mirth.<br>Come now, what masques, what dances shall we<br>have<br>To ease the anguish of a torturing hour? 20<br>Call Philostrate. |
| PHILOSTRATE | Here mighty [[Theseus]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Say, what abridgement have you for this evening?<br>What [[Masque\|masque]], what music? How shall we beguile<br>The lazy [[Time\|time]] if not with some delight? |
| PHILOSTRATE | There is a brief how many sports are ripe. 25<br>Make choice of which your highness will see first. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | ‘The battle with the centaurs to be sung<br>By an Athenian eunuch to the harp.’<br>We’ll none of that. That have I told my [[Love\|love]]<br>In glory of my kinsman Hercules. 30<br>‘The riot of the tipsy Bacchanals<br>Tearing the Thracian singer in their rage.’<br>That is an old device, and it was played<br>When I from Thebes came last a conquerer.<br>‘The thrice-three Muses mourning for the [[Death\|death]] 35<br>Of learning, late deceased in beggary.’<br>That is some [[Satire\|satire]], keen and critical,<br>Not sorting with a nuptial ceremony.<br>‘A tedious brief scene of young Pyramus<br>And his [[Love\|love]] Thisby.’ ’Tedious’ and ‘brief’? 40 |
| PHILOSTRATE | A play there is, my lord, some ten words long,<br>Which is as ‘brief as I have known a play;<br>But by ten words, my lord, it is too long,<br>Which makes it ’tedious’; for in all the play<br>There is not one word apt, one player fitted. 45 |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | What are they that do play it? |
| PHILOSTRATE | Hard-handed men that work in Athens here,<br>Which never laboured in their minds till now,<br>And now have toiled their unbreathed memories<br>With this same play against your nuptial. 50 |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Go, bring them in; and take your places, ladies. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Philostrate |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | I [[Love\|love]] not to see wretchedness o’ercharged<br>And duty in his service perishing. |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | A PLAY called The Troublesome Reign of John, [[King]] of England, published anonymously in 1591, has sometimes been thought to be a derivative version of Shakespeare’s [[King John]], first published in the 1623 Folio; more probably Shakespeare wrote his play in 1595 or 1596, using The Troublesome Reign—itself based on Holinshed’s Chronicles and John Foxe’s Book of Martyrs (1563)—as his principal source. Like [[Richard II]], [[King John]] is written entirely in verse.<br>[[King John]] (c.1167―1216) was famous as the opponent of papal tyranny, and The Troublesome Reign is a violently anti-Catholic play; but Shakespeare is more moderate. He portrays selected events from John’s reign—like The Troublesome Reign, making no mention of Magna Carta—and ends with John’s [[Death\|death]], but John is not so dominant a figure in his play as [[Richard II]] or [[Richard III]] in theirs. Indeed, the longest—and liveliest—role is that of [[Richard]] Coeur-de-[[Lion\|lion]]’s illegitimate son, Philip Falconbridge, the Bastard.<br>[[King John]]’s reign was troublesome initially because of his weak claim to his brother [[Richard]] Coeur-de-[[Lion\|lion]]’s throne. Prince [[Arthur]], son of John’s elder brother Geoffrey, had no less strong a claim, which is upheld by his mother, [[Constance]], and by [[King Philip of France]]. The waste and futility of the consequent war between power-hungry leaders is satirically demonstrated in the dispute over the French town of Angers, which is resolved by a [[Marriage\|marriage]] between John’s niece, Lady Blanche of Spain, and Louis, the French [[Dauphin]]. The moral is strikingly drawn by the Bastard—the man best fitted to be [[King\|king]], but debarred by accident of birth—in his speech (2.1.562-99) on ’commodity’ ([[Self\|self]]-interest). [[King]] Philip breaks his treaty with England, and in the ensuing battle Prince [[Arthur]] is captured. He becomes the play’s [[Touchstone\|touchstone]] of humanity as he persuades John’s agent, [[Hubert]], to disobey John’s orders to blind him, only to kill himself while trying to escape. John’s noblemen, thinking the [[King]] responsible for the boy’s [[Death\|death]], defect to the French, but return to their allegiance on learning that the [[Dauphin]] intends to kill them after conquering England. John dies, poisoned by a monk; the play ends with the reunited noblemen swearing allegiance to John’s son, the young Henry III, and with the Bastard’s boast that<br>This England never did, nor never shall,<br>Lie at the proud foot of a conqueror<br>But when it first did help to wound itself.<br>Twentieth-century revivals of [[King John]] were infrequent, but it was popular in the nineteenth century, when the roles of the [[King]], the Bastard, and [[Constance]] all appealed to successful actors; a production of 1823 at Covent [[Garden]] inaugurated a trend for historically accurate settings and costumes which led to a number of spectacular revivals. |
| THE PERSONS OF THE | PLAY |
| [[King John\|KING JOHN]] | of England |
| [[Queen\|QUEEN]] | [[Eleanor\|ELEANOR]], his mother |
| LADY FALCONBRIDGE | [[Philip the Bastard\|Philip the BASTARD]], later knighted as Sir [[Richard]] Plantagenet,<br>her illegitimate son by [[King]] [[Richard]] I (Coeur-de-[[Lion\|lion]])<br>Robert FALCONBRIDGE, her legitimate son<br>James GURNEY, her attendant<br><br>Lady BLANCHE of Spain, niece of [[King John]] |
| PRINCE | HENRY, son of [[King John]]<br>[[Hubert\|HUBERT]], a follower of [[King John]]<br>[[Earl of Salisbury\|Earl of SALISBURY]]<br>Earl of PEMBROKE<br>Earl of ESSEX<br>Lord BIGOT |
| [[King\|KING]] PHILIP | of France |
| LOUIS THE | [[Dauphin\|DAUPHIN]], his son<br>[[Arthur\|ARTHUR]], Duke of Brittaine, nephew of [[King John]]<br>Lady [[Constance\|coNSTANCE]], his mother<br>Duke of AUSTRIA (Limoges)<br>CHÂTILLON, ambassador from France to England<br>Count MELUN<br><br>A CITIZEN of Angers<br>Cardinal PANDOLF, a legate from the Pope |
| PETER OF | POMFRET, a [[Prophet\|prophet]] |
| SHERIFF | Lords, soldiers, attendants |




<span class="atom-split" data-atom="act_1--scene_1" data-title="A Midsummer Nights Dream — Act I, Scene 1" data-chapter="Scene 1" data-kind="scene" data-tags="archetype/child,archetype/shadow,motif/blood,motif/cross,motif/death,motif/desire,motif/dreams,motif/fire,motif/lightning,motif/love,motif/moon,motif/night,motif/pain,motif/rose,motif/soul,motif/tears,motif/time,motif/well,motif/window,concept/beauty,concept/history,concept/melancholy,concept/self,form/history,character/demetrius,character/diana,character/helen,character/helena,character/hermia,character/hippolyta,character/lysander,character/phoebe,character/queen,character/speed,character/theseus,character/venus"></span>


| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Theseus]], [[Hippolyta]], and Philostrate, with others |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Now, fair [[Hippolyta]], our nuptial hour<br>Draws on apace. Four happy days bring in<br>Another [[Moon\|moon]]—but O, methinks how slow<br>This old [[Moon\|moon]] wanes! She lingers my desires<br>Like to a stepdame or a dowager<br>Long withering out a young man’s revenue. |
| [[Hippolyta\|HIPPOLYTA]] | Four days will quickly steep themselves in [[Night\|night]],<br>Four nights will quickly dream away the [[Time\|time]];<br>And then the [[Moon\|moon]], like to a silver bow<br>New bent in heaven, shall behold the [[Night\|night]]<br>Of our solemnities. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Go, Philostrate,<br>Stir up the Athenian youth to merriments.<br>Awake the pert and nimble spirit of mirth.<br>Turn [[Melancholy\|melancholy]] forth to funerals—<br>The pale companion is not for our pomp. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Philostrate |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | [[Hippolyta]], I wooed thee with my [[Sword\|sword]],<br>And won thy [[Love\|love]] doing thee injuries.<br>But I will wed thee in another key—<br>With pomp, with triumph, and with revelling. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Egeus and his daughter [[Hermia]], and [[Lysander]] and [[Demetrius]] |
| EGEUS | Happy be [[Theseus]], our renowned Duke. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Thanks, good Egeus. What’s the news with thee? |
| EGEUS | Full of vexation come I, with complaint<br>Against my [[Child\|child]], my daughter [[Hermia]].—<br>Stand forth [[Demetrius]].—My noble lord,<br>This man hath my consent to marry her.—<br>Stand forth [[Lysander]].—And, my gracious Duke,<br>This hath bewitched the bosom of my [[Child\|child]].<br>Thou, thou, [[Lysander]], thou hast given her rhymes,<br>And interchanged [[Love\|love]] tokens with my [[Child\|child]].<br>Thou hast by moonlight at her [[Window\|window]] sung<br>With feigning voice verses of feigning [[Love\|love]],<br>And stol’n the impression of her fantasy<br>With bracelets of thy hair, rings, gauds, conceits,<br>Knacks, trifles, nosegays, sweetmeats—messengers<br>Of strong prevailment in unhardened youth. 35<br>With cunning hast thou filched my daughter’s heart,<br>Turned her obedience which is due to me<br>To stubborn harshness. And, my gracious Duke,<br>Be it so she will not here before your grace<br>Consent to marry with [[Demetrius]],<br>I beg the ancient privilege of Athens:<br>As she is mine, I may dispose of her,<br>Which shall be either to this gentleman<br>Or to her [[Death\|death]], according to our law<br>Immediately provided in that case. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | What say you, [[Hermia]]? Be advised, fair maid.<br>To you your father should be as a god,<br>One that composed your beauties, yea, and one<br>To whom you are but as a form in wax,<br>By him imprinted, and within his power<br>To leave the figure or disfigure it.<br>[[Demetrius]] is a worthy gentleman. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | So is [[Lysander]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | In himself he is,<br>But in this kind, wanting your father’s voice,<br>The other must be held the worthier. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | I would my father looked but with my eyes. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Rather your eyes must with his judgement look. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | I do entreat your grace to pardon me.<br>I know not by what power I am made bold,<br>Nor how it may concern my modesty<br>In such a presence here to plead my thoughts,<br>But I beseech your grace that I may know<br>The worst that may befall me in this case<br>If I refuse to wed [[Demetrius]]. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Either to die the [[Death\|death]], or to abjure<br>For ever the society of men.<br>Therefore, fair [[Hermia]], question your desires.<br>Know of your youth, examine [[Well\|well]] your [[Blood\|blood]],<br>Whether, if you yield not to your father’s choice,<br>You can endure the livery of a nun,<br>For aye to be in shady cloister mewed,<br>To live a barren sister all your life,<br>Chanting faint hymns to the cold fruitless [[Moon\|moon]].<br>Thrice blessed they that master so their [[Blood\|blood]]<br>To undergo such maiden pilgrimage;<br>But earthlier happy is the [[Rose\|rose]] distilled<br>Than that which, withering on the virgin thorn,<br>Grows, lives, and dies in single blessedness. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | So will I grow, so live, so die, my lord,<br>Ere I will yield my virgin patent up<br>Unto his lordship whose unwishèd yoke<br>My [[Soul\|soul]] consents not to give sovereignty. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | Take [[Time\|time]] to pause, and by the next new [[Moon\|moon]]—<br>The sealing day betwixt my [[Love\|love]] and me<br>For everlasting bond of fetlowship—<br>Upon that day either prepare to die<br>For disobedience to your father’s will,<br>Or else to wed [[Demetrius]], as he would,<br>Or on [[Diana]]’s altar to protest<br>For aye austerity and single life. |
| [[Demetrius\|DEMETRIUS]] | Relent, sweet [[Hermia]]; and, [[Lysander]], yield<br>Thy crazed title to my certain right. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | You have her father’s [[Love\|love]], [[Demetrius]];<br>Let me have [[Hermia]]’s. Do you marry him. |
| EGEUS | Scornful [[Lysander]]! True, he hath my [[Love\|love]];<br>And what is mine my [[Love\|love]] shall render him,<br>And she is mine, and all my right of her<br>I do estate unto [[Demetrius]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] to [[Theseus]] |
| EGEUS | I am, my lord, as [[Well\|well]] derived as he,<br>As [[Well\|well]] possessed. My [[Love\|love]] is more than his,<br>My fortunes every way as fairly ranked,<br>If not with vantage, as [[Demetrius]];<br>And—which is more than all these boasts can be—<br>I am beloved of beauteous [[Hermia]].<br>Why should not I then prosecute my right?<br>[[Demetrius]]—I’ll avouch it to his head-<br>Made [[Love\|love]] to Nedar’s daughter, [[Helena]],<br>And won her [[Soul\|soul]], and she, sweet lady, dotes,<br>Devoutly dotes, dotes in idolatry<br>Upon this spotted and inconstant man. |
| [[Theseus\|THESEUS]] | I must confess that I have heard so much,<br>And with [[Demetrius]] thought to have spoke thereof;<br>But, being over-full of [[Self\|self]] affairs,<br>My mind did lose it. But, [[Demetrius]], come;<br>And come, Egeus. You shall go with me.<br>I have some private schooling for you both.<br>For you, fair [[Hermia]], look you arm yourself<br>To fit your fancies to your father’s will,<br>Or else the law of Athens yields you up—<br>Which by no means we may extenuate—<br>To [[Death\|death]] or to a vow of single life.<br>Come, my [[Hippolyta]]; what cheer, my [[Love\|love]]?—<br>[[Demetrius]] and Egeus, go along.<br>I must employ you in some business<br>Against our nuptial, and confer with you<br>Of something nearly that concerns yourselves. |
| EGEUS | With duty and [[Desire\|desire]] we follow you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt all but [[Lysander]] and [[Hermia]] |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | How now, my [[Love\|love]]? Why is your cheek so pate ?<br>How chance the roses there do fade so fast? |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | Belike for want of rain, which I could [[Well\|well]]<br>Beteem them from the tempest of my eyes. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Ay me, for aught that I could ever read,<br>Could ever hear by tale or [[history]],<br>The course of true [[Love\|love]] never did run smooth,<br>But either it was different in btood— |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | O [[Cross\|cross]]!—too high to be enthralled to low. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Or else misgrafted in respect of years— |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | O spite!—too old to be engaged to young. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Or merit stood upon the choice of friends— |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | O hell!—to choose [[Love\|love]] by another’s eyes. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Or if there were a sympathy in choice,<br>War, [[Death\|death]], or sickness did lay siege to it,<br>Making it momentany as a sound,<br>Swift as a [[Shadow\|shadow]], short as any dream,<br>Brief as the [[Lightning\|lightning]] in the collied [[Night\|night]],<br>That, in a spleen, unfolds both heaven and earth,<br>And, ere a man hath power to say ‘Behold!’<br>The jaws of darkness do devour it up.<br>So quick bright things come to confusion. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | If then true [[lovers]] have been ever crossed,<br>It stands as an edict in destiny.<br>Then let us teach our trial patience,<br>Because it is a customary [[Cross\|cross]],<br>As due to [[Love\|love]] as thoughts, and [[Dreams\|dreams]], and sighs,<br>Wishes, and [[Tears\|tears]], poor fancy’s followers. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | A good [[Persuasion\|persuasion]]. Therefore hear me, [[Hermia]].<br>I have a widow aunt, a dowager<br>Of great revenue, and she hath no [[Child\|child]],<br>And she respects me as her only son.<br>From Athens is her [[house]] remote seven leagues.<br>There, gentle [[Hermia]], may I marry thee,<br>And to that place the sharp Athenian law<br>Cannot pursue us. If thou lov’st me then,<br>Steal forth thy father’s [[house]] tomorrow [[Night\|night]],<br>And in the wood, a league without the town,<br>Where I did meet thee once with [[Helena]]<br>To do observance to a morn of May,<br>There will I stay for thee. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | My good [[Lysander]],<br>I swear to thee by Cupid’s strongest bow,<br>By his best arrow with the golden head,<br>By the simplicity of [[Venus]]’ doves,<br>By that which knitteth souls and prospers loves,<br>And by that [[Fire\|fire]] which burned the Carthage [[Queen\|queen]]<br>When the false Trojan under sail was seen;<br>By all the vows that ever men have broke—<br>In number more than ever women spoke—<br>In that same place thou hast appointed me<br>Tomorrow truly will I meet with thee. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | Keep promise, [[Love\|love]]. Look, here comes [[Helena]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Helena]] |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | God [[Speed\|speed]], fair [[Helena]]. Whither away? |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | Call you me fair? That ’fair’ again unsay.<br>[[Demetrius]] loves your fair—O happy fair!<br>Your eyes are lodestars, and your tongue’s sweet air<br>More tuneable than lark to shepherd’s ear<br>When wheat is green, when hawthorn buds appear.<br>Sickness is catching. O, were favour so!<br>Your words I catch, fair [[Hermia]]; ere I go,<br>My ear should catch your voice, my eye your eye,<br>My tongue should catch your tongue’s sweet melody.<br>Were the world mine, [[Demetrius]] being bated,<br>The rest I’d give to be to you translated.<br>O, teach me how you look, and with what art<br>You sway the motion of [[Demetrius]]’ heart. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | I frown upon him, yet he loves me still. |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | O that your frowns would teach my smiles such skill! |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | I give him curses, yet he gives me [[Love\|love]]. |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | O that my prayers could such affection move! |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | The more I hate, the more he follows me. |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | The more I [[Love\|love]], the more he hateth me. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | His folly, [[Helen]], is no fault of mine. |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | None but your [[Beauty\|beauty]]; would that fault were mine! |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | Take comfort. He no more shall see my face.<br>[[Lysander]] and myself will fly this place.<br>Before the [[Time\|time]] I did [[Lysander]] see<br>Seemed Athens as a paradise to me.<br>O then, what graces in my [[Love\|love]] do dwell,<br>That he hath turned a heaven unto a hell? |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | [[Helen]], to you our minds we will unfold.<br>Tomorrow [[Night\|night]], when [[Phoebe]] doth behold<br>Her silver visage in the wat’ry glass,<br>Decking with liquid pearl the bladed grass—<br>A [[Time\|time]] that [[lovers]]’ sleights doth still conceal—<br>Through Athens’ gates have we devised to steal. |
| [[Hermia\|HERMIA]] | And in the wood where often you and I<br>Upon faint primrose beds were wont to lie,<br>Emptying our bosoms of their counsel sweet,<br>There my [[Lysander]] and myself shall meet,<br>And thence from Athens turn away our eyes<br>To seek new friends and stranger companies.<br>Farewell, sweet playfellow. Pray thou for us,<br>And good luck grant thee thy [[Demetrius]].—<br>Keep word, [[Lysander]]. We must starve our sight<br>From [[lovers]]’ food till morrow deep midnight. |
| [[Lysander\|LYSANDER]] | I will, my [[Hermia]]. Exit [[Hermia]]<br>[[Helena]], adieu.<br>As you on him, [[Demetrius]] dote on you. Exit |
| [[Helena\|HELENA]] | How happy some o’er other some can be!<br>Through Athens I am thought as fair as she.<br>But what of that? [[Demetrius]] thinks not so.<br>He will not know what all but he do know.<br>And as he errs, doting on [[Hermia]]’s eyes,<br>So I, admiring of his qualities.<br>Things base and vile, holding no quantity,<br>[[Love]] can transpose to form and dignity.<br>[[Love]] looks not with the eyes, but with the mind,<br>And therefore is winged Cupid painted blind.<br>Nor hath [[Love\|love]]’s mind of any judgement taste;<br>Wings and no eyes figure unheedy haste.<br>And therefore is [[Love\|love]] said to be a [[Child\|child]]<br>Because in choice he is so oft beguiled.<br>As waggish boys in game themselves forswear,<br>So the boy [[Love]] is perjured everywhere.<br>For ere [[Demetrius]] looked on [[Hermia]]’s eyne<br>He hailed down oaths that he was only mine,<br>And when this hail some heat from [[Hermia]] felt,<br>So he dissolved, and showers of oaths did melt.<br>I will go tell him of fair [[Hermia]]’s flight.<br>Then to the wood will he tomorrow [[Night\|night]]<br>Pursue her, and for this intelligence<br>If I have thanks it is a dear expense.<br>But herein mean I to enrich my [[Pain\|pain]],<br>To have his sight thither and back again. Exit |
